class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False

            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
                undefinedFunction(word)
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

LeetCode 2781: "Length of the Longest Valid Substring" You are given a string word and a list forbidden. A string is valid if no substring is forbidden. Return length of longest valid substring. Constraints: forbidden[i].length <= 10, word.length <= 1e5. There is a known sliding window solution:
```
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        forbidden = set(forbidden)
        ans = 0
        left = 0
        for right in range(len(word)):
            for i in range(right, max(right-10, left-1), -1):
                if word[i:right+1] in forbidden:
                    left = i + 1
                    break
            ans = max(ans, right - left + 1)
        return ans
```
This checks all substrings ending at right up to length 10. Another trie solution maybe:
```
trie = {}
for f in forbidden: ...
def find(s):
    node = trie
    for i, c in enumerate(s):
        if c not in node: return -1
        node = node[c]
        if 'end' in node: return i+1
    return -1
...
```

def find_forbidden_start(i, j):
    node = trie
    for pos in range(i, min(j, i+max_len)):
        if word[pos] not in node: return None
        node = node[word[pos]]
        if "end" in node:
            return pos - i + 1
    return None
```

Let's define f(i) = max r (exclusive) such that word[i:r] valid. For i from n down to 0, f(n)=n. For i < n:
- Let L_i = length of shortest forbidden word that is a prefix of word[i:] (if any).
- If L_i exists, then any r > i+L_i-1 includes that forbidden, so r <= i+L_i-1.
- Also word[i:r] valid implies word[i+1:r] valid, so r <= f(i+1).
- Thus f(i) = min(f(i+1), i+L_i-1) if L_i exists, else f(i+1).
This matches algorithm if `isForbidden(word[i:f(i+1)])` returns L_i when L_i <= f(i+1)-i. But what if L_i exists but L_i > f(i+1)-i? Then the forbidden word starting at i extends beyond f(i+1), so it is not fully contained in word[i:f(i+1)]. But could there be a valid substring starting at i with r > f(i+1)? No, because r <= f(i+1) from right boundary. So no need to consider L_i. The algorithm's isForbidden on word[i:f(i+1)] would return False if the path doesn't reach end before f(i+1), so j remains f(i+1). Correct. If L_i <= f(i+1)-i, returns L_i. Good.

But wait: What if there is a forbidden word starting at i of length L_i, but the shortest forbidden prefix of word[i:f(i+1)] is not L_i because the path to L_i goes through characters but there is a shorter forbidden word that is not a prefix of word[i:]? Impossible, if shorter forbidden word is a prefix of word[i:], it is also a prefix of word[i:f(i+1)] if its length <= f(i+1)-i. If its length > f(i+1)-i, it's not fully in s. So isForbidden returns the shortest forbidden prefix that fits within s. That's exactly min(L_i, f(i+1)-i? Actually if L_i <= f(i+1)-i, returns L_i. If L_i > f(i+1)-i, returns False. Good.

Thus algorithm correct.

Option 1: Keep `isForbidden(s)` but avoid slicing by using a helper that takes word, start, end:
```
def forbidden_len_at(i, j):
    node = trie
    for pos in range(i, j):
        if word[pos] not in node:
            return 0
        node = node[word[pos]]
        if "end" in node:
            return pos - i + 1
    return 0
```
Then main:
```
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    truc = forbidden_len_at(i, j)
    if truc:
        j = i + truc - 1
    res = max(res, j - i)
```
This avoids slicing. But note: If `j` is large and trie path continues for a long forbidden word, loop may go until j. Since forbidden length <=10, but if we don't know, could be up to n if forbidden list contains a word of length n? Constraints forbid length <=10, but to be safe, we can stop when `pos - i >= max_len`? Actually if we traverse until j, and trie path has no end but continues because there is a forbidden word longer than j-i? If j-i can be large, and there is a forbidden word of length 100, loop 100. Fine. But if forbidden word length can be n, loop n per i, O(n^2) again. But constraints say <=10. To be robust, we can precompute max_len = max(len(f) for f in forbidden) and limit range to min(j, i+max_len). Because no forbidden word longer than max_len, so no need to check beyond i+max_len. This is efficient and correct. If forbidden empty, max_len=0, but then loop range empty; we can handle.

However, if we limit to i+max_len, and `j` is less than i+max_len, use min(j, i+max_len). Good.

But wait: The algorithm's invariant uses f(i+1) as j. If we only check up to max_len, and there is a forbidden word of length L <= max_len starting at i, it will be found if L <= j-i. If L > j-i, not fully in window, no need. Good.

def forbidden_len(i, j):
    node = trie
    limit = min(j, i + max_len)

        c = word[pos]
        if c not in node:

```
If max_len=0, limit=i, range empty, returns 0. Good.

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def forbidden_len(i: int, j: int) -> int:
            node = trie
            limit = min(j, i + max_len)
            for pos in range(i, limit):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in node:
                    return 0
                node = node[c]
                if "end" in node:
                    return pos - i + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = forbidden_len(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Need check if `max_len` is 0, `i + max_len` = i, limit = min(j, i) = i, range empty. Good.

i=0 j=3 res=3. Good.

Full code:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(i: int, j: int) -> int:
            node = trie
            limit = min(j, i + max_len)
            for pos in range(i, limit):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in node:
                    return 0
                node = node[c]
                if "end" in node:
                    return pos - i + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Let's search memory of LeetCode 2781 trie solution. There is a known solution:
```
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        forbidden = set(forbidden)
        ans = 0
        left = 0
        for right in range(len(word)):
            for i in range(right, max(right-10, left-1), -1):
                if word[i:right+1] in forbidden:
                    left = i + 1
                    break
            ans = max(ans, right-left+1)
        return ans
```
This is simpler. The faulty code's backward trie is unusual but correct.

Let's see if there is a bug in the original code's `isForbidden` that returns `counter` but if the forbidden word is a prefix of `s`, and there is a shorter forbidden word that is also a prefix, it returns the shorter. Good. But what if the shorter forbidden word is not a prefix of `s` because `s` starts with a longer word that has the shorter word as a prefix? Wait if the shorter word is a prefix of the longer word, and `s` starts with the longer word, then `s` also starts with the shorter word. So yes.

Potential issue: The original code's `isForbidden` returns `False` if `c not in t`. But if `s` has a forbidden word as a prefix, but the trie path has an "end" at a node, and then there are more characters in `s`, it returns at the end. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `j` update should be `j = i + truc` instead of `i + truc - 1` if `j` is inclusive? But `j` is used as exclusive in slicing and res. Initially j=len(word), so exclusive. If forbidden length L, to exclude the forbidden word from substring starting at i, the maximum exclusive end is i+L-1 (includes first L-1 chars). So correct. Let's verify with "ab" forbidden, word="ab". If j=i+L-1=1, res=1, valid substring "a". If j=i+L=2, res=2, invalid. So -1 correct.

Potential issue: The code's `res = max(res, j - i)` after update. If no forbidden, j-i is length of word[i:j]. If forbidden, j-i = L-1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the helper's `limit = min(j, i + max_len)` should be `min(j, i + max_len)` or `min(j, i + max_len + 1)`? We need check positions up to i+L-1 inclusive. If max_len = L, and j > i+L, we need check pos = i+L-1. range(i, i+L) includes i+L-1. So limit = i+max_len. Good. If max_len=0, range empty. Good.

Potential issue: If `j` is exclusive and equals i+max_len, and forbidden word length max_len starts at i, then it is fully within word[i:j] because j = i+max_len, includes indices i..i+max_len-1. The helper limit = min(j, i+max_len) = i+max_len, range includes i+max_len-1. Good.

Potential issue: If `j` is less than i+max_len, limit=j, range includes up to j-1. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `trie` with "end" key could conflict with a character named "end" if word contains "end" as a string? No, word characters are single characters, "end" is a multi-character string, so no conflict. If word could contain multi-character tokens, not in problem.

But wait, what if the shorter forbidden word is not actually a substring because it is a prefix of a longer word and the shorter word is in forbidden, but the shorter word is followed by a character that makes it not a separate token? Substring doesn't require token boundaries. So "a" is a substring of "ab". Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `trie` with "end" key could conflict with a character named "end" if word contains "end" as a string? No, word characters are single characters, "end" is a multi-character string, so no conflict. If word could contain multi-character tokens, not in problem.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug in the original code's `isForbidden` that returns `counter` but if the forbidden word is at the beginning of `s` and there is a longer forbidden word that is a prefix, the shorter one is returned. We said correct. But wait, what if the shorter forbidden word is not actually a substring because it is a prefix of a longer word and the shorter word is in forbidden, but the shorter word is followed by a character that makes it not a separate token? Substring doesn't require token boundaries. So "a" is a substring of "ab". Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `trie` with "end" key could conflict with a character named "end" if word contains "end" as a string? No, word characters are single characters, "end" is a multi-character string, so no conflict. If word could contain multi-character tokens, not in problem.

But what if there is a valid substring starting at i-1 that includes char i? It would contain forbidden char i, so invalid. Good.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
options. The user asked for fixed code only. The code has an undefined function and uses slicing inefficiently. I will output the corrected, efficient version.</think>```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        max_len = 0

        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def forbidden_len(i: int, j: int) -> int:
            node = trie
            limit = min(j, i + max_len)
            for pos in range(i, limit):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in node:
                    return 0
                node = node[c]
                if "end" in node:
                    return pos - i + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)

        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = forbidden_len(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)

        return res
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        max_len = 0

        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def forbidden_len(i: int, j: int) -> int:
            node = trie
            limit = min(j, i + max_len)
            for pos in range(i, limit):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in node:
                    return 0
                node = node[c]
                if "end" in node:
                    return pos - i + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)

        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = forbidden_len(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)

        return res
